Still 35 pages. The external modification to section5_scierc_draft.tex (adding the Bonferroni correction sentence) is the main culprit here. Let me check if the Bonferroni sentence can be merged with the existing text to save space. Let me re-read the current composability paragraph.

The new L105-108:
```
Fine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\% but leaves residual violations (Table~\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp (removed triples are 97.6--100\% false positives).


```

Now I'm looking at the bootstrap significance testing results. The paired bootstrap analysis with 10,000 resamples shows that after applying Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons, none of the R-F1 differences reach statistical significance, with the minimum p-value at 0.014 and all 95% confidence intervals staying within a narrow range. The composability factor drops substantially from the zero-shot setting to the fine-tuned model, and beam search with k=4 is also being evaluated.

I notice the Bonferroni correction details are already in the table caption, so having them repeated in the body text creates redundancy. I should consider removing the statistical details from the main text and keeping them only in the caption to avoid duplication.

Looking at the next part, LoRA successfully eliminates all four violation types on the SciERC dataset, achieving R-F1 scores of 43.60 and 46.18.

``` I'm merging these two sentences to eliminate the redundancy between the body text and caption. The key insight is combining the general result (DR reduction and R-F1 range) with the statistical validation (bootstrap tests and Bonferroni correction) into one cohesive statement, rather than stating the findings twice with slightly different levels of detail.

Now looking at the DocRED footnote—it's quite lengthy and explains a technical edge case about subword tokens from different enum entries combining in an unusual way.  I can compress this significantly by removing the specific example and just noting that it's a rare automaton edge case that's been reported upstream, which cuts the footnote down to roughly a third of its original length and recovers another line or so of space.

    "originalFile": "% Section 5 — DocRED Results (compressed, 1450→~600 words)\n% exp_ids: phase2_docred_unconstrained, phase2_docred_grammar_only,\n%          phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1\n% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Dataset Validation on DocRED}\n\\label{sec:docred-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:docred-ablation} presents the four-way ablation on DocRED (96 Wikidata relation types, 14$\\times$ SciERC; Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, 998 documents).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: DocRED four-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{6}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-7} \\cmidrule(l){8-9}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF & Tot.\\,$\\downarrow$\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 18.46 & ---\n  & 29.66 & 2.48\n  & 0.10  & 18.56\n  & 7.74  & 58.15 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 26.17 & $\\uparrow$42\\%\n  & \\textbf{0.03} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.07  & 26.24\n  & \\textbf{8.31}  & \\textbf{59.38} \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 13.84 & $\\downarrow$25\\%\n  & 29.87 & 4.22\n  & 1.05  & 14.89\n  & 7.70  & 57.81 \\\\\nFull (S1)\n  & \\textbf{10.21} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{45\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0.03} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 2.57  & \\textbf{12.78}\n  & 8.23  & 59.37 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Four-way ablation on DocRED (998 documents, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ unconstrained.\nTot.\\ = DR + CF (total type-violation burden).\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded; Tot.\\ included).\nF1 $\\times$100. This has been reported upstream and is not a fundamental limitation of the approach.}\nR-F1 improves from 7.74 to 8.23 ($+$0.50\\,pp; $p < 0.001$), attributable to elimination of 5{,}189 OOV relation predictions.\nCF increases from 0.10\\% to 2.57\\%, consistent with the CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nResidual zero-shot DR (10.21\\%) traces to grammar-first fallback under tight constraints ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$; \\S\\ref{sec:greedy-limitation}); causal tracing confirms all residual DR occurs at fallback positions (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing}).\n\n% exp_ids: phase2_docred_grammar_only, phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1\n\\paragraph{Layer contributions.}\nGrammar-only eliminates RV and ET but \\emph{increases} DR to 26.17\\%: vocabulary restriction makes OOV outputs evaluable, exposing latent type incompatibilities.\nSemantic-only reduces DR by 25\\% but cannot eliminate RV or ET, the same empirical enabling dependency observed on SciERC.\nResidual DR (10.21\\% vs.\\ 0.93\\% on SciERC) reflects high $\\bar{\\kappa}$ (0.842) and \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity; fine-tuning narrows DR to $\\leq$\\,0.09\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}), with the 36 residual violations decomposing into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), incorrect relation selection (17\\%), and direction reversal (11\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\n",

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Director is coordinating final revisions of an EMNLP 2026 paper "OntoCoDec" about type-constrained IE via compiled ontology constraints, with a May 25 deadline. This session handled multiple sequential Patch directives:
   - **Page overflow fix**: Recovering from 35→34 pages after Dream-review-driven revisions pushed the Conclusion off page 8
   - **CF bound unification**: Checking all FT CF% values and unifying the bound across the paper (CF≤1.3%)
   - **Trie baseline data update**: Updating R-F1 numbers after violation_checker.py parse failure bug fix (Qwen 42.57→41.16, Llama 41.42→19.95)
   - **Trie baseline narrative strengthening**: Rewriting the constrained baselines paragraph to use R-F1 + parse failure comparison (per Reviewer Report #70), while maintaining 8-page limit

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - OntoCoDec: Two-layer constrained decoding (grammar + semantic) for IE
   - Grammar layer: CFG-based JSON structure + vocabulary membership
   - Semantic layer: Prefix-decidable with state, runtime entity registry
   - DR (Domain/Range) violations vs CF (Cross-Field) violations
   - Violation conversion: DR enforcement converts to CF; fine-tuning resolves
   - Trie-only baseline (GCD-equivalent): vocabulary membership without JSON structural enforcement
   - Parse failure rates: model-dependent (Llama 50.8% vs Qwen 9.1% under trie-only)
   - EMNLP 2026: 8-page main text limit, unlimited references/appendix
   - Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons (4 FT settings)

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **section5_runtime.tex** (Most heavily modified this session)
     - Contains runtime overhead, beam search, 70B evaluation, and constrained baselines paragraphs
     - Current state of constrained baselines paragraph (L21-26):
     ```latex
     \paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}
     \label{sec:constrained-baselines}
     Our grammar-only ablation (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same constraints as GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}: RV/ET$\to$0\% but only 3\% DR reduction vs.\ Full S1's 94\%.
     Without JSON structural enforcement, trie-only vocabulary constraints yield R-F1 19.95--41.16 on SciERC LoRA (vs.\ OntoCoDec's 43.77--45.47) with 9.1--50.8\% parse failure (Appendix~\ref{app:trie-baseline}); the model-dependent disparity confirms grammar enforcement provides a model-independent structural guarantee.
     BoostCD's~\cite{boostcd2025} boosting is complementary and potentially integrable with OntoCoDec.
     ```
     - Beam search paragraph compressed (removed setup sentence "explores paths satisfying both layers jointly")
     - 70B ZS sentence compressed (removed +0.09pp, +0.04pp, "(from ≤0.07%)")
     - Current 70B paragraph:
     ```latex
     On Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4), zero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets: SciERC 6.41\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 11.24), DocRED 14.98\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 13.05); CF rises to 3.50--7.01\%, comparable to 8B zero-shot CF.
     ```
     - 70B LoRA DocRED still has XX.XX placeholders

   - **section5_scierc_draft.tex**
     - Externally modified during session: added Bonferroni correction sentence + updated table caption

     ```latex
     Fine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\% but leaves residual violations (Table~\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% with no significant $\Delta$R-F1 after Bonferroni correction (min.\ $p{=}0.014$; 95\% CIs $\subseteq [-0.76, {+}0.22]$\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\% false positives).
     ```

        "content": "% Section 5 — DocRED Results (compressed, 1450→~600 words)\n% exp_ids: phase2_docred_unconstrained, phase2_docred_grammar_only,\n%          phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1\n% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Dataset Validation on DocRED}\n\\label{sec:docred-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:docred-ablation} presents the four-way ablation on DocRED (96 Wikidata relation types, 14$\\times$ SciERC; Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, 998 documents).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: DocRED four-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{6}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-7} \\cmidrule(l){8-9}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF & Tot.\\,$\\downarrow$\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 18.46 & ---\n  & 29.66 & 2.48\n  & 0.10  & 18.56\n  & 7.74  & 58.15 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 26.17 & $\\uparrow$42\\%\n  & \\textbf{0.03} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.07  & 26.24\n  & \\textbf{8.31}  & \\textbf{59.38} \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 13.84 & $\\downarrow$25\\%\n  & 29.87 & 4.22\n  & 1.05  & 14.89\n  & 7.70  & 57.81 \\\\\nFull (S1)\n  & \\textbf{10.21} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{45\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0.03} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 2.57  & \\textbf{12.78}\n  & 8.23  & 59.37 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Four-way ablation on DocRED (998 documents, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ unconstrained.\nTot.\\ = DR + CF (total type-violation burden).\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded; Tot.\\ included).\nF1 $\\times$100. Bootstrap significance: R-F1 $+$0.50\\,pp ($p < 0.001$).\nGrammar-only $\\uparrow$DR: vocabulary enforcement makes OOV outputs evaluable, exposing latent violations (see text).}\n\\label{tab:docred-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% exp_ids: phase2_docred_unconstrained, phase2_docred_full_s1\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\nFull S1 reduces DR from 18.46\\% to 10.21\\% ($\\downarrow$45\\%), eliminates ET ($\\to$\\,0\\%), and eliminates RV (29.66\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.03\\%).\\footnote{Residual 0.03\\% RV traces to a rare XGrammar automaton edge case (chimeric subword tokens across enum entries); reported upstream.}\nR-F1 improves from 7.74 to 8.23 ($+$0.50\\,pp; $p < 0.001$), attributable to elimination of 5{,}189 OOV relation predictions.\nCF increases from 0.10\\% to 2.57\\%, consistent with the CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nResidual zero-shot DR (10.21\\%) traces to grammar-first fallback under tight constraints ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$; \\S\\ref{sec:greedy-limitation}); causal tracing confirms all residual DR occurs at fallback positions (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing}).\n\n% exp_ids: phase2_docred_grammar_only, phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1\n\\paragraph{Layer contributions.}\nGrammar-only eliminates RV and ET but \\emph{increases} DR to 26.17\\%: vocabulary restriction makes OOV outputs evaluable, exposing latent type incompatibilities.\nSemantic-only reduces DR by 25\\% but cannot eliminate RV or ET, the same empirical enabling dependency observed on SciERC.\nResidual DR (10.21\\% vs.\\ 0.93\\% on SciERC) reflects high $\\bar{\\kappa}$ (0.842) and \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity; fine-tuning narrows DR to $\\leq$\\,0.09\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}), with the 36 residual violations decomposing into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), incorrect relation selection (17\\%), and direction reversal (11\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\n",

        "content": "% Section 5 — SciERC Results (Draft)\n% Data source: Phase 2 SciERC registry experiments\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_grammar_only,\n%          phase2_scierc_semantic_only, phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_scierc_full_s2\n% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.\n\n\\subsection{Ablation and Composability with Fine-Tuning}\n\\label{sec:scierc-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} presents the five-way ablation on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, 551 documents); only the active constraint layers differ across rows.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: SciERC five-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{6}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-7} \\cmidrule(l){8-9}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF & Tot.\\,$\\downarrow$\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 15.37 & ---\n  & 1.39  & 0.40\n  & 0.08  & 15.45\n  & 8.23  & 31.01 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 14.84 & $\\downarrow$3\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.08  & 14.92\n  & 8.23  & 30.98 \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 11.65 & $\\downarrow$24\\%\n  & 1.51  & 0.40\n  & 2.71  & 14.36\n  & 8.15  & 31.01 \\\\\nFull (S1)\n  & \\textbf{0.93} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{94\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 9.90  & \\textbf{10.83}\n  & \\textbf{8.36} & 30.98 \\\\\nFull (S2)\n  & 0.97  & $\\downarrow$94\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{9.00}\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{31.90} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 docs, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ unconstrained.\nTot.\\ = DR + CF (total type-violation burden).\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded; Tot.\\ included).\nF1 $\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.\nCF: same entity assigned conflicting types across triples.\nS1 = single-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}), S2 = two-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:s2}).\nGrammar-only represents the ceiling of grammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}: vocabulary membership is enforced but cross-field semantic constraints are not.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nFull S1 reduces DR from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\% ($\\downarrow$94\\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\\,$\\to$\\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\%, reaching 0\\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 48\\% of predictions rather than correcting them (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).\nTotal type-violation burden (Tot.\\,=\\,DR+CF) decreases from 15.45\\% to 10.83\\% ($\\downarrow$30\\%), reflecting \\emph{violation conversion}: DR enforcement replaces ontologically invalid triples with individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF 0.08\\%\\,$\\to$\\,9.90\\%); under fine-tuning, Tot.\\ drops to 1.18\\% with CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.3\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Layer contributions.}\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823\nGrammar-only eliminates RV and ET with no F1 impact while DR remains at 14.84\\%, the ceiling of grammar-based decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}; grammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\\%), so CF originates from the semantic layer.\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% and cannot eliminate RV or ET; this is the \\emph{empirical enabling dependency}: adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 and CF; we adopt S1.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_qwen_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\n% exp_ids: bootstrap_ci_ft_composability.json\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% with no significant $\\Delta$R-F1 after Bonferroni correction (min.\\ $p{=}0.014$; 95\\% CIs $\\subseteq [-0.76, {+}0.22]$\\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\\% false positives).\nCF drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned; Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) with LoRA eliminates all four violation types on SciERC (R-F1 43.60, 46.18; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.56 & 0.08 & 40.89 & 82.35 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0.00} & 0.22 & 40.79 & 81.87 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 44.55 & 84.77 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.00 & 0.26 & 44.70 & 84.71 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nF1\\,$\\times$100.\nPaired bootstrap (B${=}$10{,}000, document-level): no $\\Delta$R-F1 reaches significance after Bonferroni correction ($\\alpha/4{=}0.0125$; min.\\ $p{=}0.014$; all 95\\% CIs $\\subseteq [-0.76, {+}0.22]$\\,pp); DR reduction is significant in all settings ($p{<}0.001$).\nE-F1 includes parse-failure documents (Qwen PF: 7--8\\%; Llama PF: 2--3\\%).}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (f) Downstream impact of DR elimination\n%  exp_ids: task_e_lucky_violation_analysis\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Downstream impact.}\n\\label{sec:downstream-impact}\nAmong constraint-removed triples, only 0.49\\% match gold labels on average (``lucky violations''), confirming $<$0.5\\% recall cost; on Llama SciERC all five are recovered via legal type paths (zero churn; Appendix~\\ref{app:lucky-violations}).\n",
        "numLines": 154,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 154
      }
    },

        "content": "% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)\n\n\\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}\n\\label{sec:runtime}\nS1 overhead is $+$13.1\\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\\% with 37\\% lower absolute latency.\nConstraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\n\n\\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}\n\\label{sec:beam-results}\nWith LoRA, beam search ($k{=}4$)~\\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on SciERC (R-F1 44--46); zero-shot beam achieves DR${=}$0\\% on both datasets (CF response is model-dependent; Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\nTotal latency: 1.54$\\times$ (beam 1.32$\\times$ $+$ constraint 1.17$\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:beam-latency}).\n\n% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200\n\\paragraph{70B evaluation.}\n\\label{sec:70b-results}\nOn Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4), zero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets: SciERC 6.41\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% (R-F1 11.24), DocRED 14.98\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% (R-F1 13.05); CF rises to 3.50--7.01\\%, comparable to 8B zero-shot CF.\n% TODO: 待 70B LoRA DocRED 200-doc + CF 数据回填\nLoRA fine-tuning confirms scale-independent composability: SciERC DR 1.50\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% (R-F1 44.53, $-$0.20\\,pp); DocRED (200-document subset) DR XX.XX\\%\\,$\\to$\\,XX.XX\\% (R-F1 XX.XX); CF drops from 3.50\\% (zero-shot) to 1.33\\% (LoRA), consistent with the 8B pattern (9.90\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.18\\%; \\S\\ref{sec:composability}).\n\n% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc\n\\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}\n\\label{sec:constrained-baselines}\nOur grammar-only ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same constraints as GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}: RV/ET$\\to$0\\% but only 3\\% DR reduction vs.\\ Full S1's 94\\%.\nWithout JSON structural enforcement, trie-only vocabulary constraints yield R-F1 19.95--41.16 on SciERC LoRA (vs.\\ OntoCoDec's 43.77--45.47) with 9.1--50.8\\% parse failure (Appendix~\\ref{app:trie-baseline}); the model-dependent disparity confirms grammar enforcement provides a model-independent structural guarantee.\nBoostCD's~\\cite{boostcd2025} boosting is complementary and potentially integrable with OntoCoDec.\n",
        "numLines": 26,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 26
      }
    },

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_full_ft.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Full Fine-Tuning Baseline}\n\\label{app:full-ft}\n\nTo verify that constraint effectiveness generalizes beyond LoRA, we evaluate Qwen3-8B with full-parameter fine-tuning on SciERC (same data, 3 epochs, lr=$2{\\times}10^{-4}$, seed 42).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\textbf{E-F1} & \\textbf{R-F1} & \\textbf{DR\\%} & \\textbf{CF\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull FT unconstrained & 64.78 & 37.89 & 2.74 & 0 \\\\\nFull FT + S1 & 64.78 & 37.89 & \\textbf{0} & 1.31 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLoRA unconstrained & 66.86 & 43.65 & 0.31 & 0 \\\\\nLoRA + S1 & 67.05 & 43.77 & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Full fine-tuning vs.\\ LoRA on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, 551 docs). Constraints eliminate DR under both methods. Full FT underperforms LoRA, likely due to an untuned learning rate ($2{\\times}10^{-4}$, standard for LoRA but high for full FT). F1 is unaffected by constraints in both cases.}\n\\label{tab:full-ft}\n\\end{table}\n\nFull fine-tuning retains 2.74\\% DR violations (30 triples), concentrated in Part-of (14.6\\%), Hyponym-of (9.6\\%), and Evaluate-for (8.4\\%). S1 eliminates all 30 violations with no F1 cost, which confirms that constraint enforcement is effective regardless of the fine-tuning method. The CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off persists (CF 0\\%$\\to$1.31\\%), consistent with the LoRA pattern (1.18\\%).\n",
        "numLines": 25,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 25
      }
    },

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01URSfHD9Yfe22t7Wfq411zN`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	We provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.
5	
6	\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}
7	XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\cite{willard2023outlines}, SynCode~\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and related systems~\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql,park2024grammar,jsonschemabench2025} enforce format validity via CFG-based token masking.
8	NeuroLogic Decoding~\cite{lu2021neurologic} enforces predicate-logic constraints via beam-level reranking; OntoCoDec applies constraints per-token via forward masking, avoiding beam-width dependence.
9	In code generation, PICARD~\cite{scholak2021picard} and \citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata, but IE type environments accumulate dynamically (\S\ref{sec:composition}).
10	
11	\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}
12	GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie}, KnowCoder~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}, GUIDEX~\cite{guidex2025}, GenIE~\cite{josifoski2022genie}, TANL~\cite{paolini2021tanl}, REBEL~\cite{cabot2021rebel}, InstructUIE~\cite{wang2023instructuie}, and others~\cite{fei2022lasuie,wang2022deepstruct,lu2022uie,lou2023usm,zhou2024universalner} encode ontology structure or frame IE as structured generation, though schema compliance gaps persist~\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.
13	GoLLIE and KnowCoder embed schema knowledge via code-style prompts, achieving structural compliance without runtime enforcement; GoLLIE-7B eliminates DR via its dataclass format but at severe coverage cost (R-F1${=}$0.0, 46\% empty output), while OntoCoDec with LoRA achieves R-F1${=}$45.47 at DR${=}$0.09\%, surpassing KnowCoder-7B (supervised R-F1${=}$40.0)~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}.
14	The paradigms are complementary: GoLLIE constrains output format, OntoCoDec constrains content generation.
15	
16	\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}
17	\citet{roth_yih_2004} and \citet{riedel2010modeling} formulated hard type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation~\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} detects violations after generation but discards up to 48\% of predictions including true positives.
18	EnriCo~\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} uses ASP; IterGen~\cite{ugare2025itergen} backtracks on violations---OntoCoDec uses forward-only masking at $O(|V|)$ per step, avoiding backtracking latency.
19	Grammar-only enforcement (GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd}, BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}) achieves only 3\% DR reduction (\S\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}); DCCD~\cite{dccd2026} explores draft-conditioned constrained decoding.
20	Extraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\cite{tam2024letmespeak} despite degradation in other domains~\cite{banerjee2025crane,formattax2026}.
21	
```

</details>

```text
60	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_unconstrained, phase2_docred_full_s1
61	\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}
62	Full S1 reduces DR from 18.46\% to 10.21\% ($\downarrow$45\%), eliminates ET ($\to$\,0\%), and eliminates RV (29.66\%\,$\to$\,0.03\%).\footnote{Residual 0.03\% RV traces to a rare XGrammar automaton edge case (chimeric subword tokens across enum entries); reported upstream.}
63	R-F1 improves from 7.74 to 8.23 ($+$0.50\,pp; $p < 0.001$), attributable to elimination of 5{,}189 OOV relation predictions.
64	CF increases from 0.10\% to 2.57\%, consistent with the CF$\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
65	Residual zero-shot DR (10.21\%) traces to grammar-first fallback under tight constraints ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$; \S\ref{sec:greedy-limitation}); causal tracing confirms all residual DR occurs at fallback positions (Appendix~\ref{app:causal-tracing}).
66	
67	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_grammar_only, phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1
68	\paragraph{Layer contributions.}
69	Grammar-only eliminates RV and ET but \emph{increases} DR to 26.17\%: vocabulary restriction makes OOV outputs evaluable, exposing latent type incompatibilities.
70	Semantic-only reduces DR by 25\% but cannot eliminate RV or ET, the same empirical enabling dependency observed on SciERC.
71	Residual DR (10.21\% vs.\ 0.93\% on SciERC) reflects high $\bar{\kappa}$ (0.842) and \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity; fine-tuning narrows DR to $\leq$\,0.09\% (Table~\ref{tab:composability}), with the 36 residual violations decomposing into entity type errors (39\%), schema coverage gaps (33\%), incorrect relation selection (17\%), and direction reversal (11\%; Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).
72

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "GoLLIE and KnowCoder embed schema knowledge via code-style prompts, achieving structural compliance without runtime enforcement; GoLLIE-7B eliminates DR via its dataclass format but at severe coverage cost (R-F1${=}$0.0, 46\\% empty output), while OntoCoDec with LoRA achieves R-F1${=}$45.47 at DR${=}$0.09\\%, surpassing KnowCoder-7B (supervised R-F1${=}$40.0)~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}.\nThe paradigms are complementary: GoLLIE constrains output format, OntoCoDec constrains content generation.",
    "newString": "GoLLIE and KnowCoder embed schema knowledge via code-style prompts, achieving structural compliance without runtime enforcement; GoLLIE-7B eliminates DR via its dataclass format but at severe coverage cost (R-F1${=}$0.0, 46\\% empty output), while OntoCoDec with LoRA achieves R-F1${=}$45.47 at DR${=}$0.09\\%, surpassing KnowCoder-7B (supervised R-F1${=}$40.0)~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nWe provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}\nXGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\\cite{willard2023outlines}, SynCode~\\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and related systems~\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql,park2024grammar,jsonschemabench2025} enforce format validity via CFG-based token masking.\nNeuroLogic Decoding~\\cite{lu2021neurologic} enforces predicate-logic constraints via beam-level reranking; OntoCoDec applies constraints per-token via forward masking, avoiding beam-width dependence.\nIn code generation, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard} and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata, but IE type environments accumulate dynamically (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}\nGoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie}, KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}, GUIDEX~\\cite{guidex2025}, GenIE~\\cite{josifoski2022genie}, TANL~\\cite{paolini2021tanl}, REBEL~\\cite{cabot2021rebel}, InstructUIE~\\cite{wang2023instructuie}, and others~\\cite{fei2022lasuie,wang2022deepstruct,lu2022uie,lou2023usm,zhou2024universalner} encode ontology structure or frame IE as structured generation, though schema compliance gaps persist~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.\nGoLLIE and KnowCoder embed schema knowledge via code-style prompts, achieving structural compliance without runtime enforcement; GoLLIE-7B eliminates DR via its dataclass format but at severe coverage cost (R-F1${=}$0.0, 46\\% empty output), while OntoCoDec with LoRA achieves R-F1${=}$45.47 at DR${=}$0.09\\%, surpassing KnowCoder-7B (supervised R-F1${=}$40.0)~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}.\nThe paradigms are complementary: GoLLIE constrains output format, OntoCoDec constrains content generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}\n\\citet{roth_yih_2004} and \\citet{riedel2010modeling} formulated hard type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} detects violations after generation but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives.\nEnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} uses ASP; IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} backtracks on violations---OntoCoDec uses forward-only masking at $O(|V|)$ per step, avoiding backtracking latency.\nGrammar-only enforcement (GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd}, BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}) achieves only 3\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}); DCCD~\\cite{dccd2026} explores draft-conditioned constrained decoding.\nExtraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\\cite{tam2024letmespeak} despite degradation in other domains~\\cite{banerjee2025crane,formattax2026}.\n",

Tot.\\ included).\nF1 $\\times$100. phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nFull S1 reduces DR from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\% ($\\downarrow$94\\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\\,$\\to$\\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\%, reaching 0\\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 48\\% of predictions rather than correcting them (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).\nTotal type-violation burden (Tot.\\,=\\,DR+CF) decreases from 15.45\\% to 10.83\\% ($\\downarrow$30\\%), reflecting \\emph{violation conversion}: DR enforcement replaces ontologically invalid triples with individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF 0.08\\%\\,$\\to$\\,9.90\\%); under fine-tuning, Tot.\\ drops to 1.18\\% with CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.3\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Layer contributions.}\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823\nGrammar-only eliminates RV and ET with no F1 impact while DR remains at 14.84\\%, the ceiling of grammar-based decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}; grammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\\%), so CF originates from the semantic layer.\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% and cannot eliminate RV or ET; this is the \\emph{empirical enabling dependency}: adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 and CF; we adopt S1.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_qwen_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\n% exp_ids: bootstrap_ci_ft_composability.json\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% with no significant $\\Delta$R-F1 after Bonferroni correction (min.\\ $p{=}0.014$; 95\\% CIs $\\subseteq [-0.76, {+}0.22]$\\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\\% false positives).\nCF drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned; Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) with LoRA eliminates all four violation types on SciERC (R-F1 43.60, 46.18; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.56 & 0.08 & 40.89 & 82.35 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0.00} & 0.22 & 40.79 & 81.87 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 44.55 & 84.77 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.00 & 0.26 & 44.70 & 84.71 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nF1\\,$\\times$100.\nPaired bootstrap (B${=}$10{,}000, document-level): no $\\Delta$R-F1 reaches significance after Bonferroni correction ($\\alpha/4{=}0.0125$; min.\\ $p{=}0.014$; all 95\\% CIs $\\subseteq [-0.76, {+}0.22]$\\,pp); DR reduction is significant in all settings ($p{<}0.001$).\nE-F1 includes parse-failure documents (Qwen PF: 7--8\\%; Llama PF: 2--3\\%).}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (f) Downstream impact of DR elimination\n%  exp_ids: task_e_lucky_violation_analysis\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Downstream impact.}\n\\label{sec:downstream-impact}\nAmong constraint-removed triples, only 0.49\\% match gold labels on average (``lucky violations''), confirming $<$0.5\\% recall cost;     "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

```text
1	% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)
2	
3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency.
6	Constraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
7	
8	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
9	\label{sec:beam-results}
10	With LoRA, beam search ($k{=}4$)~\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on SciERC (R-F1 44--46); zero-shot beam achieves DR${=}$0\% on both datasets (CF response is model-dependent; Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
11	Total latency: 1.54$\times$ (beam 1.32$\times$ $+$ constraint 1.17$\times$; Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency}).
12	
13	% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200
14	\paragraph{70B evaluation.}
15	\label{sec:70b-results}
16	On Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4), zero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets: SciERC 6.41\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 11.24), DocRED 14.98\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 13.05); CF rises to 3.50--7.01\%, comparable to 8B zero-shot CF.
17	% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained, eval_70b_lora_docred_200_s1_constrained
18	LoRA fine-tuning confirms scale-independent composability: SciERC DR 1.50\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 44.53, $-$0.20\,pp); DocRED (200-document subset; Appendix~\ref{app:power-analysis}) DR 0.22\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 43.95, $+$0.15\,pp); CF drops from 3.50\% (zero-shot) to 0.18--1.33\% (LoRA), consistent with the 8B pattern (9.90\%\,$\to$\,1.18\%; \S\ref{sec:composability}).
19	
20	% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc
21	\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}
22	\label{sec:constrained-baselines}
23	Our grammar-only ablation (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same constraints as GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}: RV/ET$\to$0\% but only 3\% DR reduction vs.\ Full S1's 94\%.
24	Without JSON structural enforcement, trie-only vocabulary constraints yield R-F1 19.95--41.16 on SciERC LoRA (vs.\ OntoCoDec's 43.77--45.47) with 9.1--50.8\% parse failure (Appendix~\ref{app:trie-baseline}); the model-dependent disparity confirms grammar enforcement provides a model-independent structural guarantee.
25	BoostCD's~\cite{boostcd2025} boosting is complementary and potentially integrable with OntoCoDec.
26	
```

68	% === Limitations (ARR requirement: standalone section, does not count toward page limit) ===
69	\clearpage
70	\section*{Limitations}
71	
72	\paragraph{Constraint scope.}
73	CF impact is model-dependent: for well-calibrated models (Qwen, Llama; DR\,$\leq$\,1\% under greedy constrained decoding), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\%\,$\to$\,1.27\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.
74	CF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\,pp). CF$\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).
75	Global entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.
76	CF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.
77	Entity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\,pp for Qwen vs.\ $-$0.64\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\% vs.\ 6.8\%).
78	Residual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\%), schema coverage gaps (33\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).
79	Schema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) arise because RE constraints are extracted from training-data type pairs, which may not cover all valid combinations; larger training sets or manual constraint table completion can reduce this gap.
80	
81	\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}
82	Zero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\% compared to $\sim$45\% for fine-tuned systems.
83	Zero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on 8B-class (two models, full datasets) and 70B-class models (one model; SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).
84	Scaling to smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern's predictive scope may differ at other scales.
85	Composability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%, R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp; EE: RoV$\to$0\%, AC$\leq$0.8\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\%, Llama 0.46\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.
86	WikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).
87	All main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\ref{app:sampling},~\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.
88	
89	\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}
90	Scalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\,s; Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.
91	Our approach requires access to model logits for token-level masking, which excludes API-only models (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) where only output text is available; extending to API-only settings would require alternative enforcement mechanisms such as constrained sampling APIs or output reranking.
92	Continuation masking assumes each entity type name maps to a distinguishable token prefix sequence.
93	This assumption holds for our evaluation ontologies but may be challenged by ontologies with morphologically similar type names (e.g., \texttt{Protein} vs.\ \texttt{Protein\_Complex} vs.\ \texttt{Protein\_Domain}); when type names share a common prefix longer than one token, masking granularity depends on the tokenizer's subword segmentation.
94	Our evaluation is limited to English-language datasets; the continuation masking mechanism may behave differently for languages without whitespace-delimited tokenization (e.g., Chinese, Japanese), which we leave to future work.
95	Fine-tuning composability was validated exclusively with LoRA; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective (DR 2.74\%\,$\to$\,0\%; Appendix~\ref{app:full-ft}); other fine-tuning methods may exhibit different constraint interaction patterns and warrant further investigation.
96	
97	
98	% === Ethics Statement ===
99	\section*{Ethics Statement}
100	
101	This work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.
102	No human subjects were involved in the experiments.
103	The constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.
104	Code and data will be released upon acceptance.

larger training sets or manual constraint table completion can reduce this gap.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}\nZero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\\% compared to $\\sim$45\\% for fine-tuned systems.\nZero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on 8B-class (two models, full datasets) and 70B-class models (one model; SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern's predictive scope may differ at other scales.\nComposability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp; EE: RoV$\\to$0\\%, AC$\\leq$0.8\\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\\%, Llama 0.46\\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.\nWikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}\nScalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\\,s; Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.\nOur approach requires access to model logits for token-level masking, which excludes API-only models (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) where only output text is available; extending to API-only settings would require alternative enforcement mechanisms such as constrained sampling APIs or output reranking.\nContinuation masking assumes each entity type name maps to a distinguishable token prefix sequence.\nThis assumption holds for our evaluation ontologies but may be challenged by ontologies with morphologically similar type names (e.g., \\texttt{Protein} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Complex} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Domain}); when type names share a common prefix longer than one token, masking granularity depends on the tokenizer's subword segmentation.\nOur evaluation is limited to English-language datasets; the continuation masking mechanism may behave differently for languages without whitespace-delimited tokenization (e.g., Chinese, Japanese), which we leave to future work.\nFine-tuning composability was validated exclusively with LoRA; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective (DR 2.74\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}); other fine-tuning methods may exhibit different constraint interaction patterns and warrant further investigation.\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === ARR Responsible NLP Research Checklist ===\n\\input{checklist}\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\\input{appendix_scalability}\n\\input{appendix_full_ft}\n\\input{appendix_tcr}\n\\input{appendix_kappa_case_study}\n\\input{appendix_pumping_lemma}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

DR\\,$\\leq$\\,1\\% under greedy constrained decoding), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.27\\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.\nCF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\\,pp). our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}\nScalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\\,s; Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.\nOur approach requires access to model logits for token-level masking, which excludes API-only models (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) where only output text is available; extending to API-only settings would require alternative enforcement mechanisms such as constrained sampling APIs or output reranking.\nContinuation masking assumes each entity type name maps to a distinguishable token prefix sequence.\nThis assumption holds for our evaluation ontologies but may be challenged by ontologies with morphologically similar type names (e.g., \\texttt{Protein} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Complex} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Domain}); when type names share a common prefix longer than one token, masking granularity depends on the tokenizer's subword segmentation.\nOur evaluation is limited to English-language datasets; the continuation masking mechanism may behave differently for languages without whitespace-delimited tokenization (e.g., Chinese, Japanese), which we leave to future work.\nFine-tuning composability was validated exclusively with LoRA; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective (DR 2.74\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}); other fine-tuning methods may exhibit different constraint interaction patterns and warrant further investigation.\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === ARR Responsible NLP Research Checklist ===\n\\input{checklist}\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\\input{appendix_scalability}\n\\input{appendix_full_ft}\n\\input{appendix_tcr}\n\\input{appendix_kappa_case_study}\n\\input{appendix_pumping_lemma}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 75,
        "oldLines": 6,
        "newStart": 75,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " Global entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.",
          " CF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.",
          " Entity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\\,pp for Qwen vs.\\ $-$0.64\\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\\% vs.\\ 6.8\\%).",
          "+On 70B DocRED LoRA, parse failure rises modestly under S1 (10.0\\%\\,$\\to$\\,12.5\\%) due to generation timeouts on long documents rather than JSON format errors; SciERC (shorter documents) shows 0\\% parse failure in both settings.",
          " Residual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).",
          " Schema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) arise because RE constraints are extracted from training-data type pairs, which may not cover all valid combinations; larger training sets or manual constraint table completion can reduce this gap.",

### Tool result

_source line 3841 · 2026-05-22T10:25:53.722Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DMBRGNWju23vyzZrSx2ZYg`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
3	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \emph{constraint tightness} ($\bar{\kappa}$), a descriptive metric correlating schema restrictiveness with enforcement outcomes ($n{=}3$ schemas).
4	The two layers exhibit an empirical enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves this reduction alone.
5	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
6	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning, \textsc{OntoCoDec} achieves deployment-grade type compliance: DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% at R-F1 43--46\% ($\pm$0.6\,pp, $<$0.5\% true-positive loss); residual CF ($\leq$1.3\%) represents \emph{violation conversion}---ontologically invalid triples (DR) are replaced by individually valid but type-inconsistent ones---resolved by beam search on SciERC (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
7	Zero-shot evaluation serves as a diagnostic, revealing dataset- and calibration-dependent effectiveness (45--99\% DR reduction, total violation reduction 31\%; \S\ref{sec:experiments}) and establishing constraint tightness ($\bar{\kappa}$) as a predictive factor.
8	The framework is validated on RE (SciERC, DocRED) with preliminary event extraction results on WikiEvents, across five autoregressive LMs (7--70B; 70B zero-shot and LoRA in \S\ref{sec:70b-results}) with logits access and three tokenizer families (up to 96 relation types).
9	\end{abstract}
10	
```

Let me verify the key numbers across abstract, conclusion, and experiments:

**Abstract claims:**
- DR ≤ 0.09% → section5_scierc_draft L106: "DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%" ✅
- R-F1 43-46% → section5_scierc_draft Table 3: Qwen 43.77, Llama 45.47 ✅

Looking at the actual deltas from the experiments—-0.09, -0.10, +0.02, +0.15—the maximum absolute change is only 0.15pp, so ±0.6pp must be referring to the confidence interval bounds rather than observed differences. The CI spans from -0.76 to +0.22, giving a maximum deviation of 0.76pp, which ±0.6pp seems to approximate as a rounded average of the CI range across the four settings. Looking at the full statement from section 5, the confidence interval envelope of [-0.76, +0.22] pp is what the abstract rounds to ±0.6pp, though that's a bit loose since the negative bound exceeds 0.6. Checking the actual performance deltas across the models—like Llama SciERC showing +0.02pp and Qwen at 43.86—the ±0.6pp appears to be a rough summary rather than a precise characterization of the observed changes. The ±0.6pp notation in the abstract might be a symmetric approximation that doesn't quite match the actual CI bounds—the lower bound of -0.76pp exceeds that range. This could be an outdated number from before the latest Bonferroni analysis, but that's not what I'm being asked to fix here. The consistency checker flagged a parsing issue with missing experiment sections, not numerical discrepancies. Let me focus on verifying the key metrics across sections: the DR threshold of ≤0.09% and CF threshold of ≤1.3% both check out in the abstract, conclusion, and experiments. The ±0.6pp bound appears to be a deliberate simplification for the abstract, even though the worst confidence interval extends to -0.76pp — likely because the precise CIs were added later during Bonferroni analysis. I'll note this discrepancy to the Director without making unilateral changes, then move on to compiling the final report and checking the build log for any undefined reference warnings.

72	\paragraph{Constraint scope.}
73	CF impact is model-dependent: for well-calibrated models (Qwen, Llama; DR\,$\leq$\,1\% under greedy constrained decoding), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\%\,$\to$\,1.27\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.
74	CF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\,pp). CF$\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).
75	Global entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.
76	CF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.
77	Entity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\,pp for Qwen vs.\ $-$0.64\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\% vs.\ 6.8\%).
78	On 70B DocRED LoRA, parse failure rises modestly under S1 (10.0\%\,$\to$\,12.5\%) due to generation timeouts on long documents rather than JSON format errors; SciERC (shorter documents) shows 0\% parse failure in both settings.
79	Residual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\%), schema coverage gaps (33\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).
80	Schema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) arise because RE constraints are extracted from training-data type pairs, which may not cover all valid combinations; larger training sets or manual constraint table completion can reduce this gap.
81	
```

[Director 指令] **Round 17 审稿修复 — P0+P1 批次**

距 EMNLP 2026 ARR DDL 5/25 仅 3 天。以下修改按优先级排序，逐条执行。每条修改后确认涉及文件和行号。

---

### P0: 数字修正（必须精确）

**MF4: "five" → "four" autoregressive LMs**
- abstract.tex L8: "five autoregressive LMs" → "four autoregressive LMs"
- 全文搜索 "five" 在 LM 计数上下文的其他出现，全部改为 "four"

**MF2: CF bound ≤1.3% → ≤1.4%**
- abstract.tex L6: "≤1.3%" → "≤1.4%"
- section5_scierc_draft.tex: 搜索 "1.3%" 在 CF bound 上下文，改为 "1.4%"
- section1_introduction_draft.tex: 同上
- main.tex Limitations: 同上
- section7_conclusion_compressed.tex: 同上

**MF1: DR 范围统一**
- abstract.tex L7: "45--99% DR reduction" → "42--99% DR reduction"
- section1_introduction_draft.tex: "DR drops 76--99% for calibrated models" → 需要修正。实际数据：SciERC 88-99%, DocRED 42-90%。"calibrated models" (Qwen, Llama) 在 DocRED 上分别为 45% 和 90%。改为 "DR drops 42--99% across models and datasets, with 88--99% on SciERC"
- section5_crossmodel_draft.tex: 确认 "76--99%" 出现位置并同步修正

**MF4 补充: Limitations "three 8B-class and one 70B-class model" 已正确（=4），无需改**

---

### P0: CF 叙事修正

**MF3: Abstract 区分 ZS/FT CF**
- abstract.tex: 当前 "residual CF (≤1.4%)" 仅限 FT。加一句或半句说明 ZS CF 范围更大。
  改写建议：原 "residual CF ($\leq$1.4\%) represents \emph{violation conversion}" → "residual CF ($\leq$1.4\% fine-tuned; up to 12\% zero-shot) represents \emph{violation conversion}"

**MF3 补充: §5 主文**
- section5_crossmodel_draft.tex 的 ZS 讨论段应在最差 CF 数字出现时主动标出。确认 "12.02%" 已在表格和文字中。如果 §5.1 或 §5.3 的叙事中没有直接提及 ZS CF 最差情况，补 1 句。

---

### P1: Narrative 强化

**MF5 Path A: Grammar-only = GCD 等价论证**
这是**最关键的提分项**。在 §5.4（Trie/baseline comparison 段）或 §6 Related Work 中加 2-3 句显式论证：
- "Our grammar layer implements CFG-based constrained decoding equivalent to GCD (Geng et al. 2023) and BoostCD (Chen & Qi, 2024): both enforce JSON structure and vocabulary membership via token-level masking. Table 3 row 2 (grammar-only) isolates this contribution, showing RV/ET elimination but only 3% DR reduction—demonstrating that CFG enforcement alone is insufficient for type-valid IE."
- 关键：必须让 reviewer 看到 "grammar-only ablation IS the head-to-head comparison with GCD" 这个等式。

**MF5 Path C: GoLLIE 参考**
- §6 Related Work 加 1 句定位 GoLLIE：GoLLIE (Sainz et al. 2024) 通过 code generation 内化 schema，在 SciERC 上 E-F1=9.17, R-F1=0.0（46% empty docs），代表 training-time schema internalization 路线但不提供 runtime enforcement。
- 注意：GoLLIE 不是 constrained decoding baseline，定位为 "alternative approach" 而非 head-to-head。

**SF3: "deployment-grade" 软化**
- abstract.tex: "deployment-grade type compliance" → "near-complete DR elimination"
- section1_introduction_draft.tex: 同上

**SF1: κ̄ → descriptive metric**
- abstract.tex: "establishing constraint tightness ($\bar{\kappa}$) as a predictive factor" → "introducing constraint tightness ($\bar{\kappa}$), a descriptive metric correlating schema restrictiveness with enforcement outcomes ($n{=}3$ schemas)"

- section1_introduction_draft.tex: "neither layer alone exceeds 24% DR reduction" → "on SciERC, neither layer alone exceeds 24% DR reduction"
- abstract.tex L4 如有类似表述，同步修改

**SF6: 压缩 §3.4 S2 描述**
- section3_method_draft.tex §3.4: 从约半页压缩到 3-4 句概述。保留核心思想（two-pass: first pass extracts entities, second pass constrained RE），删除实现细节。
- 释放的空间给 MF5 Path A 的等价论证。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Vs2k82EBUvoParLrF5qecZ`</summary>

```text
1	% Section 3 — Method (Draft)
2	% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction
3	% via Compiled Ontology Constraints
4	
5	\section{Method}
6	\label{sec:method}
7	
8	\input{fig1_framework}
9	
10	We present OntoCoDec, a framework that translates IE ontologies into token-level constraints enforced during autoregressive generation.
11	The design is guided by a decidability analysis of IE type constraints.
12	
13	\paragraph{Constraint decidability taxonomy.}
14	\label{sec:decidability}
15	We classify IE type constraints into three levels: (1)~\emph{context-free}: vocabulary membership and JSON structure; (2)~\emph{prefix-decidable with state}: domain/range type validity, determined at each type-bearing position given the decoded relation type and entity registry; (3)~\emph{non-local}: cross-entity type consistency, which depends on the full output.
16	Unlike code generation where types are statically declared, IE constraints fall into level~2: entity types are inferred from text, the registry is built dynamically, and constraint targets depend on the co-occurring relation.
17	OntoCoDec enforces levels 1--2 (grammar and semantic layers, respectively); level~3 is addressed by beam search or post-hoc resolution (Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
18	
19	\smallskip
20	\noindent
21	Existing grammar-constrained decoding frameworks (e.g., XGrammar) enforce level~1 constraints---structural validity and vocabulary membership---but IE domain/range rules require cross-position entity-type state that context-free grammars cannot express.
22	We formalize this gap:
23	
24	\smallskip
25	\noindent\textbf{Proposition 1} (Level 2 $\not\subseteq$ Level 1)\textbf{.}
26	\textit{Domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets are not context-free.}
27	
28	\smallskip
29	\noindent\textit{Proof sketch.}
30	Consider $\mathcal{E} = \{\tau_A, \tau_B\}$ and a relation $r$ with $\operatorname{dom}(r) = \{\tau_A\}$.
31	An output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types, followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \leq n$.
32	A PDA's LIFO stack cannot retrieve position $k < n$ without destroying entries above it; since both $n$ and $k$ are independently unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint.
33	OntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups).
34	For finite entity sets, the valid language is technically regular but the corresponding DFA requires $O(|\mathcal{E}|^{|\text{entities}|})$ states---exceeding $10^{17}$ for DocRED (7 entity types, 20+ entities per document)---making direct DFA construction infeasible and motivating the grammar-based approach (Appendix~\ref{app:pumping-lemma}).\hfill$\square$
35	
36	\noindent\textbf{Corollary} (empirical enabling dependency: a constraint whose satisfaction is a prerequisite for evaluating subsequent constraints)\textbf{.} The empirical enabling dependency is necessary: the grammar layer alone cannot enforce domain/range constraints (Proposition~1), while the semantic layer alone achieves only 24\% DR reduction without grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}, Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}).
37	\smallskip
38	
39	We describe the problem formulation (\S\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\S\ref{sec:strategies}); formal analysis of constraint composition is in Section~\ref{sec:composition}.
40	
41	
42	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
43	\label{sec:formulation}
44	
45	\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}
46	Given an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:
47	%
48	\begin{equation}\label{eq:output-schema}
49	\resizebox{.88\columnwidth}{!}{$\displaystyle
50	  y = \bigl\{\texttt{entities}: [\{n_i, \tau_i\}],\; \texttt{relations}: [\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \tau^h_j, \tau^t_j\}]\bigr\}
51	$}
52	\end{equation}
53	%
54	where each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \in \mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\tau^h_j, \tau^t_j$.
55	The output JSON Schema (\S\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\to$ tail $\to$ relation $\to$ head\_type $\to$ tail\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation, ensuring the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields and enabling sequential domain/range lookups (\S\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).
56	The grammar structurally enforces entity-before-relation ordering (100\% preservation across all configurations), ensuring the entity registry (\S\ref{sec:entity-registry}) is populated before relation-level type constraints are evaluated; unregistered entities trigger domain-only filtering.
57	
58	\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}
59	Using the ontology $\mathcal{O} = (\mathcal{E}, \mathcal{R}, \sigma)$ defined in \S\ref{sec:background}, we write $\operatorname{dom}(r) = \{\tau^h : \exists \tau^t,\, (\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h) = \{\tau^t : (\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\tau^h$.
60	Our enforcement target is \emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)$ for every triple.
61	The decoder enforces this sequentially: $\tau^h \in \operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\tau^t \in \operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h)$ at tail position, achieving best-effort pair-based enforcement (pair validity ensured when no fallback is triggered; domain-only filtering otherwise) strictly stronger than independent domain/range checks (which over-permit 33--37\% spurious pairs on SciERC/DocRED; \S\ref{sec:rectangularity}).
62	
63	
64	\subsection{Grammar Layer}
65	\label{sec:grammar}
66	
67	A JSON Schema encoding Eq.~\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \texttt{enum} constraints ($\mathcal{E}$, $\mathcal{R}$) is compiled into a pushdown automaton via XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}.
68	At step $t$, grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \subseteq V$ pass; all others receive logits $-\infty$, enforcing structural validity, vocabulary membership ($r_j \in \mathcal{R}$, $\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$), but not domain/range constraints (which depend on entity types within each triple).
69	
70	\subsection{Semantic Layer}
71	\label{sec:semantic}
72	
73	The semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.
74	
75	\paragraph{Entity registry.}
76	\label{sec:entity-registry}
77	The semantic layer builds a registry $\mathcal{D}: \text{name} \to \text{type}$ incrementally during entity generation: each completed entry $\{n_i, \tau_i\}$ adds $(n_i, \tau_i)$ to $\mathcal{D}$, which is consulted during relation generation for type lookups.
78	At each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\textsc{HeadType}, \textsc{TailType}).
79	\label{sec:position-detection}
80	
81	\paragraph{Dynamic masking.}
82	\label{sec:dynamic-masking}
83	At type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \subseteq V$.
84	At a \textsc{HeadType} position, if $h_j \in \mathcal{D}$ and $\mathcal{D}[h_j] \in \operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only the registered type; otherwise it falls back to all domain-valid types $\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$.
85	At a \textsc{TailType} position, the mask restricts to $\operatorname{range}(r_j, \tau^h_j)$ conditioned on the already-determined head type, further narrowing to the registered type when $t_j \in \mathcal{D}$.
86	This sequential check achieves best-effort pair-based enforcement (\S\ref{sec:formulation}), strictly tighter than independent domain/range checking; ``best-effort'' reflects the grammar-first fallback (\S\ref{sec:composition}), which prioritizes JSON integrity over type validity when constraints conflict.
87	
88	\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}
89	We use \emph{continuation masking} to handle context-dependent tokenizations and prefix-shared types (e.g., \textsc{Sen}/\textsc{Sid}/\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents): a compile-time prefix map identifies valid continuations for each type name, disambiguating over multiple steps across byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral).
90	
91	\paragraph{Grammar-semantic interaction.}
92	\label{sec:interaction}
93	The final mask is $G(t) \cap S(t)$; when empty, grammar-first fallback applies (\S\ref{sec:composition}), embodying the empirical enabling dependency: semantic constraints reduce DR by only 24\% without grammar-enforced vocabulary.
94	
95	
96	\subsection{Decoding Strategies}
97	\label{sec:strategies}
98	
99	\paragraph{S1 (joint extraction): Single-pass decoding.}
100	Both layers are active throughout generation; the entity registry populates during entity decoding and is immediately available for relation-level type checking.
101	
102	\paragraph{S2 (two-pass pipeline): Two-pass decoding.}\label{sec:s2}
103	A first pass extracts entities; these compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass.
104	S1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.
105	
```

</details>
